Like networking follow-ups — someone will introduce me to a contact, or I’ll meet someone who could be useful professionally, and I’ll know I should send a message, keep the conversation going, that sort of thing. But then I start thinking about all the ways it could go badly or be awkward or not lead anywhere, or that I’ll say the wrong thing, and suddenly it feels like this huge thing instead of a five-minute email. So I don’t do it, or I wait so long that it becomes embarrassing.\n\nAnd with clients, I’ve delayed deliverables, which is hard for me to say because I’ve always thought of myself as responsible and on top of things. But I’ll get stuck anticipating every possible objection, every way they might criticize it, every consequence if it’s not perfect, and instead of just finishing and sending the work, I keep revisiting it or avoiding opening it altogether. It’s not that I don’t care — it’s almost the opposite. I care so much about preventing something from going wrong that I end up making things worse.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to play soccer and see friends more, and now I’ll skip it because I’m embarrassed by how tense I am. I get irritable, and I don’t want people noticing or asking what’s wrong. So I tell myself I’m too busy or I’ll go next time, but really I’m avoiding being around people when I feel like this.\n\nDoctor: Have these overwhelming thoughts and avoidance behaviors affected your personal relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, especially with friends. I’ve definitely pulled back from people. Soccer is probably the clearest example because that used to be one of the few things that was just social and normal for me — show up, run around, see the guys, have a bit of a laugh afterward. But lately I’ve skipped it more and more because I feel tense before I even go, and I’m embarrassed by it. I worry I’ll come across as distracted or irritable, or someone will ask what’s going on and I won’t know how to answer without sounding like I’m falling apart.\n\nAnd with friends generally, I’ve been less responsive. I tell myself I’m busy or that I’ll get back to them when I’m in a better headspace, but then days go by. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I just feel like I’m not good company right now, and I hate the idea of people seeing me as this uptight, snappy version of myself.\n\nWork has fed into it too, because when I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables because I’m stuck imagining everything that could go wrong, I end up carrying that shame and stress home with me. Then I’m even less likely to reach out to friends or show up for things. So yes, it’s affecting my relationships — maybe not in one dramatic blow-up, but in this slow pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around others, even if you're not necessarily doing something that involves a lot of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. I mean… I don’t think I’ve noticed feeling anxious just from being around people in a general sense, like sitting in a room or being near others automatically sets me off.\n\nIt’s more… I’d have to think about what you mean by “around others.” If I’m just in public or around people but there’s no expectation on me, I don’t know that I’ve paid enough attention to say yes. I tend to notice it more when there’s something attached to it — like I’m supposed to respond, or show up a certain way, or people might notice that I’m tense. But just being around others, by itself… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in casual social settings where you don't have to interact or perform in any specific way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t think I’ve really separated it out that clearly in my mind — like, “am I anxious just because I’m casually around people?” versus anxious because there’s some expectation or possible judgment involved.\n\nIf I’m just somewhere casual and I truly don’t have to interact or do anything, I don’t know that I notice it as anxiety exactly. I might still be kind of aware of myself, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the people being there or because I’m already wound up going into it. It’s easier for me to recognize when there’s a chance I’ll have to talk, or someone might ask me something, or I might seem tense or off. But if there’s no real interaction or performance expected… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really paid attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, public place like a library or a park, where you don't have to interact with anyone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. A library or a park… if I’m not expected to talk to anyone or do anything, I don’t know that I would immediately label what I feel as anxiety.\n\nI might still feel a bit on edge, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’m in public or because I already arrived feeling wound up from something else. Like, if I’m sitting in a park and no one’s engaging with me, I don’t think the presence of strangers by itself is what I’ve been focusing on. Same with a library — I’m not usually thinking, “Everyone is watching me,” at least not in a clear way that I can point to.\n\nIt’s more noticeable to me when there’s some chance I’ll have to respond or be seen as not handling myself well. But in a quiet public place where there’s really no interaction expected… I don’t have a clean yes or no. I haven’t really paid close enough attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are alone with your thoughts in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually more where it happens, I think. When it’s quiet and I’m by myself, there’s nothing to distract me, so my mind just… starts running. And it doesn’t stay on one thing.\n\nLike, I’ll start with something that should be fairly contained — say a client hasn’t responded to an email or a message. At first it’s just, “Okay, why haven’t they gotten back to me?” But then within a few minutes I’m thinking maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract, maybe I missed something important, and then it turns into this whole chain of consequences. It keeps building this case against me, one step after another, and I can know on some level that I’m jumping ahead, but that doesn’t make it shut off. So yes, it’s affecting my relationships — maybe not in one dramatic blow-up, but in this slow pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around others, even if you're not necessarily doing something that involves a lot of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. I mean… I don’t think I’ve noticed feeling anxious just from being around people in a general sense, like sitting in a room or being near others automatically sets me off.\n\nIt’s more… I’d have to think about what you mean by “around others.” If I’m just in public or around people but there’s no expectation on me, I don’t know that I’ve paid enough attention to say yes. I tend to notice it more when there’s something attached to it — like I’m supposed to respond, or show up a certain way, or people might notice that I’m tense. But just being around others, by itself… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in casual social settings where you don't have to interact or perform in any specific way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t think I’ve really separated it out that clearly in my mind — like, “am I anxious just because I’m casually around people?” versus anxious because there’s some expectation or possible judgment involved.\n\nIf I’m just somewhere casual and I truly don’t have to interact or do anything, I don’t know that I notice it as anxiety exactly. I might still be kind of aware of myself, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the people being there or because I’m already wound up going into it. It’s easier for me to recognize when there’s a chance I’ll have to talk, or someone might ask me something, or I might seem tense or off. But if there’s no real interaction or performance expected… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really paid attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, public place like a library or a park, where you don't have to interact with anyone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. A library or a park… if I’m not expected to talk to anyone or do anything, I don’t know that I would immediately label what I feel as anxiety.\n\nI might still feel a bit on edge, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’m in public or because I already arrived feeling wound up from something else. Like, if I’m sitting in a park and no one’s engaging with me, I don’t think the presence of strangers by itself is what I’ve been focusing on. Same with a library — I’m not usually thinking, “Everyone is watching me,” at least not in a clear way that I can point to.\n\nIt’s more noticeable to me when there’s some chance I’ll have to respond or be seen as not handling myself well. But in a quiet public place where there’s really no interaction expected… I don’t have a clean yes or no. I haven’t really paid close enough attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are alone with your thoughts in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually more where it happens, I think. When it’s quiet and I’m by myself, there’s nothing to distract me, so my mind just… starts running. And it doesn’t stay on one thing.\n\nLike, I’ll start with something that should be fairly contained — say a client hasn’t responded to an email or a message. At first it’s just, “Okay, why haven’t they gotten back to me?” But then within a few minutes I’m thinking maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract, maybe I missed something important, and then it turns into this whole chain of consequences. If that falls through, then I’m behind financially, then I’m thinking about divorce payments, and then I’m back to thinking I’m never going to get into a real leadership role again or get my life stable the way it used to feel.\n\nIt’s like my brain won’t just stop at the first concern. It keeps building this case against me, one step after another, and I can know on some level that I’m jumping ahead, but that doesn’t make it shut off. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in — because when things get quiet, I can’t seem to turn my thoughts off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t know that I’d call it sadness exactly, and hopelessness feels like a strong word, so I don’t want to say yes if that’s not what I mean.\n\nOver the past few weeks I’ve had times where I feel pretty worn down or discouraged, but when you say “sad,” I picture something more like crying a lot or feeling grief, and that hasn’t really been what I’ve been focused on. It’s more like I get stuck in my head and then I feel heavy afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nHopelessness… I don’t know. I’ve had moments where things feel like they’re not going the way I want, or like I can’t see a simple path forward, but I’m not sure I’d describe that as feeling hopeless. Maybe frustrated, maybe overwhelmed. I’m having a hard time separating those out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you've failed in some fundamental way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it as “worthless.” That word feels… I don’t know, it feels very absolute, like I’m saying I have no value as a person, and I don’t think that’s exactly how I talk to myself.\n\nFailed in some fundamental way is harder to answer, because I do get into these loops where I look at where I thought I’d be versus where I am, and I can be pretty harsh about it. But I’m not sure if that’s the same as believing I’m fundamentally a failure, or if it’s more that I’m disappointed and I keep measuring myself against some version of my life that I thought I should have had by now.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I often feel worthless. I do feel like I’ve fallen short sometimes, but “failed in a fundamental way” sounds stronger than what I can confidently say. It’s more like questioning myself than a fixed belief, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts and avoidance of certain situations or tasks significantly impacted your daily life or relationships over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just me worrying internally and still getting everything done, but it’s started spilling over into actual things.\n\nWith work, for example, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups — not because I didn’t care or forgot entirely, but because I’d start thinking through how the conversation might go wrong, or whether I’d sound desperate, or whether they’d judge where I am professionally right now. And then I’d tell myself I’d do it later when I felt clearer, and then later became the next day, and then it just got awkward enough that I avoided it more.\n\nIt’s also affected client work. I’ve delayed deliverables because I get stuck trying to anticipate every possible objection or problem before I send something out. I’ll look at something that’s basically good enough to move forward, but then my mind starts going, “What if they hate this part? What if this creates a bigger issue? What if this makes me look incompetent?” So instead of just finishing it and communicating normally, I sit on it too long.\n\nAnd relationships, yeah. I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Soccer used to be a pretty normal outlet for me, but lately I feel tense going into it, and I’m embarrassed because I know I’ve been more irritable. I don’t really want people noticing that or asking what’s going on, so I just don’t show up as much. Same with friends — I’ve been avoiding plans or keeping things shorter, partly because I don’t want to be in that state around them.\n\nSo yes, over the past few weeks it’s definitely affected my daily life. It’s not just thoughts anymore; it’s causing me to miss things, delay things, and isolate more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s actually one of the main things that bothers me, because it doesn’t even always need a real trigger. My mind just starts moving on its own.\n\nIt’ll jump from one thing to another — like I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as, I don’t know, damaged goods professionally. And none of those things may have changed in that exact moment, but my brain treats it like there’s some emergency I’m supposed to solve right then.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not emailing back right away, can start to feel like more than just a delayed email. I’ll know logically that people get busy or hiring processes move slowly, but emotionally it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, like doors are shutting and I’m not going to be able to get back to where I should be. And then I’m off into mortgage payments again, and what happens if contract work dries up, and who has noticed, and what they think of me.\n\nSo yes, I do worry when there isn’t an obvious immediate reason. Or maybe there’s a tiny reason, but my mind turns it into a whole chain of consequences before I can stop it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep, yes. Appetite… I don’t know that I’ve noticed as clearly, but sleep has definitely changed.

Most nights lately, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I’m tired, but then my mind starts replaying things. A lot of it is interview answers or conversations I’ve had, like I’ll go back over what I said, what I should have said, whether I sounded off or unprepared. And it’s not even productive at that point — it’s just this loop.

Then I end up checking the clock, which I know doesn’t help, but I’ll see it’s 2 or 3 in the morning and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I have left, which just makes it worse. And even on nights where, technically, I get what should be enough hours, I don’t wake up feeling restored. It’s more like I spent the night on call for a system outage — like some part of me was alert the whole time, waiting for something to go wrong.

So yes, sleep has been a problem over the past few weeks. I wouldn’t say I’m just staying up late by choice. It feels more like I can’t fully shut down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0, F48.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just... I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It starts with something that should be fairly ordinary, like a client not responding to an email or a call, and then within minutes I’m way down the road in my head. I’m thinking, okay, they’re unhappy, we’re going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind financially, especially with the divorce payments, and then I start thinking I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here saying it out loud, that it sounds like a chain reaction that maybe doesn’t make sense. But in the moment it feels completely convincing, like my mind grabs one small thing and turns it into the whole future falling apart. I’ve been trying to reason with myself, but it’s like I can’t get it to stop once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when these thoughts overwhelm you?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve paid close enough attention to my body in those moments to say, like, “yes, my heart is racing” or “yes, I’m sweating.” It’s more that my attention gets locked onto the thought itself, if that makes sense — the email, the client, the worst-case scenario — and I’m sort of running the whole disaster in my head.\n\nI mean, I might be tense or keyed up, but I don’t know if I’ve noticed the specific things you mentioned, like trembling or sweating. I tend to experience it more as this mental pressure, like I’m trying to solve five problems at once and none of them are actually happening yet. So… I can’t really give you a clean answer on the physical symptoms. I just know it feels overwhelming when it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these overwhelming thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, actually.\n\nAt work, I’ve started avoiding or putting off things that used to be pretty normal parts of the job. Like networking follow-ups — someone will introduce me to a contact, or I’ll meet someone who could be useful professionally, and I’ll know I should send a message, keep the conversation going, that sort of thing. But then I start thinking about all the ways it could go badly or be awkward or not lead anywhere, or that I’ll say the wrong thing, and suddenly it feels like this huge thing instead of a five-minute email. So I don’t do it, or I wait so long that it becomes embarrassing.\n\nAnd with clients, I’ve delayed deliverables, which is hard for me to say because I’ve always thought of myself as responsible and on top of things. But I’ll get stuck anticipating every possible objection, every way they might criticize it, every consequence if it’s not perfect, and instead of just finishing and sending the work, I keep revisiting it or avoiding opening it altogether. It’s not that I don’t care — it’s almost the opposite. I care so much about preventing something from going wrong that I end up making things worse.\n\nOutside of work, I’ve pulled back too. I used to play soccer and see friends more, and now I’ll skip it because I’m embarrassed by how tense I am. I get irritable, and I don’t want people noticing or asking what’s wrong. So I tell myself I’m too busy or I’ll go next time, but really I’m avoiding being around people when I feel like this.\n\nDoctor: Have these overwhelming thoughts and avoidance behaviors affected your personal relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, especially with friends. I’ve definitely pulled back from people. Soccer is probably the clearest example because that used to be one of the few things that was just social and normal for me — show up, run around, see the guys, have a bit of a laugh afterward. But lately I’ve skipped it more and more because I feel tense before I even go, and I’m embarrassed by it. I worry I’ll come across as distracted or irritable, or someone will ask what’s going on and I won’t know how to answer without sounding like I’m falling apart.\n\nAnd with friends generally, I’ve been less responsive. I tell myself I’m busy or that I’ll get back to them when I’m in a better headspace, but then days go by. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I just feel like I’m not good company right now, and I hate the idea of people seeing me as this uptight, snappy version of myself.\n\nWork has fed into it too, because when I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables because I’m stuck imagining everything that could go wrong, I end up carrying that shame and stress home with me. Then I’m even less likely to reach out to friends or show up for things. So yes, it’s affecting my relationships — maybe not in one dramatic blow-up, but in this slow pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around others, even if you're not necessarily doing something that involves a lot of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. I mean… I don’t think I’ve noticed feeling anxious just from being around people in a general sense, like sitting in a room or being near others automatically sets me off.\n\nIt’s more… I’d have to think about what you mean by “around others.” If I’m just in public or around people but there’s no expectation on me, I don’t know that I’ve paid enough attention to say yes. I tend to notice it more when there’s something attached to it — like I’m supposed to respond, or show up a certain way, or people might notice that I’m tense. But just being around others, by itself… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in casual social settings where you don't have to interact or perform in any specific way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t think I’ve really separated it out that clearly in my mind — like, “am I anxious just because I’m casually around people?” versus anxious because there’s some expectation or possible judgment involved.\n\nIf I’m just somewhere casual and I truly don’t have to interact or do anything, I don’t know that I notice it as anxiety exactly. I might still be kind of aware of myself, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the people being there or because I’m already wound up going into it. It’s easier for me to recognize when there’s a chance I’ll have to talk, or someone might ask me something, or I might seem tense or off. But if there’s no real interaction or performance expected… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really paid attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, public place like a library or a park, where you don't have to interact with anyone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. A library or a park… if I’m not expected to talk to anyone or do anything, I don’t know that I would immediately label what I feel as anxiety.\n\nI might still feel a bit on edge, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’m in public or because I already arrived feeling wound up from something else. Like, if I’m sitting in a park and no one’s engaging with me, I don’t think the presence of strangers by itself is what I’ve been focusing on. Same with a library — I’m not usually thinking, “Everyone is watching me,” at least not in a clear way that I can point to.\n\nIt’s more noticeable to me when there’s some chance I’ll have to respond or be seen as not handling myself well. But in a quiet public place where there’s really no interaction expected… I don’t have a clean yes or no. I haven’t really paid close enough attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are alone with your thoughts in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually more where it happens, I think. When it’s quiet and I’m by myself, there’s nothing to distract me, so my mind just… starts running. And it doesn’t stay on one thing.\n\nLike, I’ll start with something that should be fairly contained — say a client hasn’t responded to an email or a message. At first it’s just, “Okay, why haven’t they gotten back to me?” But then within a few minutes I’m thinking maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract, maybe I missed something important, and then it turns into this whole chain of consequences. If that falls through, then I’m behind financially, then I’m thinking about divorce payments, and then I’m back to thinking I’m never going to get into a real leadership role again or get my life stable the way it used to feel.\n\nIt’s like my brain won’t just stop at the first concern. It keeps building this case against me, one step after another, and I can know on some level that I’m jumping ahead, but that doesn’t make it shut off. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in — because when things get quiet, I can’t seem to turn my thoughts off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t know that I’d call it sadness exactly, and hopelessness feels like a strong word, so I don’t want to say yes if that’s not what I mean.\n\nOver the past few weeks I’ve had times where I feel pretty worn down or discouraged, but when you say “sad,” I picture something more like crying a lot or feeling grief, and that hasn’t really been what I’ve been focused on. It’s more like I get stuck in my head and then I feel heavy afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nHopelessness… I don’t know. I’ve had moments where things feel like they’re not going the way I want, or like I can’t see a simple path forward, but I’m not sure I’d describe that as feeling hopeless. Maybe frustrated, maybe overwhelmed. I’m having a hard time separating those out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you've failed in some fundamental way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it as “worthless.” That word feels… I don’t know, it feels very absolute, like I’m saying I have no value as a person, and I don’t think that’s exactly how I talk to myself.\n\nFailed in some fundamental way is harder to answer, because I do get into these loops where I look at where I thought I’d be versus where I am, and I can be pretty harsh about it. But I’m not sure if that’s the same as believing I’m fundamentally a failure, or if it’s more that I’m disappointed and I keep measuring myself against some version of my life that I thought I should have had by now.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I often feel worthless. I do feel like I’ve fallen short sometimes, but “failed in a fundamental way” sounds stronger than what I can confidently say. It’s more like questioning myself than a fixed belief, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts and avoidance of certain situations or tasks significantly impacted your daily life or relationships over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just me worrying internally and still getting everything done, but it’s started spilling over into actual things.\n\nWith work, for example, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups — not because I didn’t care or forgot entirely, but because I’d start thinking through how the conversation might go wrong, or whether I’d sound desperate, or whether they’d judge where I am professionally right now. And then I’d tell myself I’d do it later when I felt clearer, and then later became the next day, and then it just got awkward enough that I avoided it more.\n\nIt’s also affected client work. I’ve delayed deliverables because I get stuck trying to anticipate every possible objection or problem before I send something out. I’ll look at something that’s basically good enough to move forward, but then my mind starts going, “What if they hate this part? What if this creates a bigger issue? What if this makes me look incompetent?” So instead of just finishing it and communicating normally, I sit on it too long.\n\nAnd relationships, yeah. I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Soccer used to be a pretty normal outlet for me, but lately I feel tense going into it, and I’m embarrassed because I know I’ve been more irritable. I don’t really want people noticing that or asking what’s going on, so I just don’t show up as much. Same with friends — I’ve been avoiding plans or keeping things shorter, partly because I don’t want to be in that state around them.\n\nSo yes, over the past few weeks it’s definitely affected my daily life. It’s not just thoughts anymore; it’s causing me to miss things, delay things, and isolate more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s actually one of the main things that bothers me, because it doesn’t even always need a real trigger. My mind just starts moving on its own.\n\nIt’ll jump from one thing to another — like I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as, I don’t know, damaged goods professionally. And none of those things may have changed in that exact moment, but my brain treats it like there’s some emergency I’m supposed to solve right then.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not emailing back right away, can start to feel like more than just a delayed email. I’ll know logically that people get busy or hiring processes move slowly, but emotionally it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, like doors are shutting and I’m not going to be able to get back to where I should be. And then I’m off into mortgage payments again, and what happens if contract work dries up, and who has noticed, and what they think of me.\n\nSo yes, I do worry when there isn’t an obvious immediate reason. Or maybe there’s a tiny reason, but my mind turns it into a whole chain of consequences before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yes. Appetite… I don’t know that I’ve noticed as clearly, but sleep has definitely changed.\n\nMost nights lately, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I’m tired, but then my mind starts replaying things. A lot of it is interview answers or conversations I’ve had, like I’ll go back over what I said, what I should have said, whether I sounded off or unprepared. And it’s not even productive at that point — it’s just this loop.\n\nThen I end up checking the clock, which I know doesn’t help, but I’ll see it’s 2 or 3 in the morning and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I have left, which just makes it worse. And even on nights where, technically, I get what should be enough hours, I don’t wake up feeling restored. It’s more like I spent the night on call for a system outage — like some part of me was alert the whole time, waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nSo yes, sleep has been a problem over the past few weeks. I wouldn’t say I’m just staying up late by choice. It feels more like I can’t fully shut down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F41.1", "F43.0", "F48.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. I mean, the clearest example is soccer, because that used to be one of the few things I could count on enjoying without overthinking it. It was just exercise, seeing people, being part of something that wasn’t work or career stuff. But lately I’ve pulled back from it. I’ll think about going, and then I start imagining myself showing up tense or snapping at someone or just seeming off, and I get embarrassed before I even get there. So instead of going and probably feeling better, I avoid it.

And it’s been similar with friends. I’ve been less willing to make plans or I keep things short, because I don’t want them to see how wound up I am. I don’t want to be the irritable guy in the room or have someone ask, “Are you okay?” and then have to explain all of it. So I just sort of disappear a bit, which I know isn’t great.

It’s also interfering with things that matter professionally. I’ve missed networking follow-ups because I get stuck imagining every way the conversation could go wrong or how I might come across. And I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep trying to anticipate every possible objection before I send something, even when it’s probably fine. So yes — it’s getting in the way of participating in things I care about, both personally and professionally.

And it’s not even productive at that point — it’s just this loop.\n\nThen I end up checking the clock, which I know doesn’t help, but I’ll see it’s 2 or 3 in the morning and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I have left, which just makes it worse. And even on nights where, technically, I get what should be enough hours, I don’t wake up feeling restored. It’s more like I spent the night on call for a system outage — like some part of me was alert the whole time, waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nSo yes, sleep has been a problem over the past few weeks. I wouldn’t say I’m just staying up late by choice. It feels more like I can’t fully shut down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your anxiety symptoms have interfered with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that are important to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the clearest example is soccer, because that used to be one of the few things I could count on enjoying without overthinking it. It was just exercise, seeing people, being part of something that wasn’t work or career stuff. But lately I’ve pulled back from it. I’ll think about going, and then I start imagining myself showing up tense or snapping at someone or just seeming off, and I get embarrassed before I even get there. So instead of going and probably feeling better, I avoid it.\n\nAnd it’s been similar with friends. I’ve been less willing to make plans or I keep things short, because I don’t want them to see how wound up I am. I don’t want to be the irritable guy in the room or have someone ask, “Are you okay?” and then have to explain all of it. So I just sort of disappear a bit, which I know isn’t great.\n\nIt’s also interfering with things that matter professionally. I’ve missed networking follow-ups because I get stuck imagining every way the conversation could go wrong or how I might come across. And I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep trying to anticipate every possible objection before I send something, even when it’s probably fine. So yes — it’s getting in the way of participating in things I care about, both personally and professionally.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll know logically that people get busy or hiring processes move slowly, but emotionally it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, like doors are shutting and I’m not going to be able to get back to where I should be. And then I’m off into mortgage payments again, and what happens if contract work dries up, and who has noticed, and what they think of me.\n\nSo yes, I do worry when there isn’t an obvious immediate reason. Or maybe there’s a tiny reason, but my mind turns it into a whole chain of consequences before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yes. Appetite… I don’t know that I’ve noticed as clearly, but sleep has definitely changed.\n\nMost nights lately, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I’m tired, but then my mind starts replaying things. A lot of it is interview answers or conversations I’ve had, like I’ll go back over what I said, what I should have said, whether I sounded off or unprepared. And it’s not even productive at that point — it’s just this loop.\n\nThen I end up checking the clock, which I know doesn’t help, but I’ll see it’s 2 or 3 in the morning and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I have left, which just makes it worse. And even on nights where, technically, I get what should be enough hours, I don’t wake up feeling restored. It’s more like I spent the night on call for a system outage — like some part of me was alert the whole time, waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nSo yes, sleep has been a problem over the past few weeks. I wouldn’t say I’m just staying up late by choice. It feels more like I can’t fully shut down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your anxiety symptoms have interfered with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that are important to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the clearest example is soccer, because that used to be one of the few things I could count on enjoying without overthinking it. It was just exercise, seeing people, being part of something that wasn’t work or career stuff. But lately I’ve pulled back from it. I’ll think about going, and then I start imagining myself showing up tense or snapping at someone or just seeming off, and I get embarrassed before I even get there. So instead of going and probably feeling better, I avoid it.\n\nAnd it’s been similar with friends. I’ve been less willing to make plans or I keep things short, because I don’t want them to see how wound up I am. I don’t want to be the irritable guy in the room or have someone ask, “Are you okay?” and then have to explain all of it. So I just sort of disappear a bit, which I know isn’t great.\n\nIt’s also interfering with things that matter professionally. I’ve missed networking follow-ups because I get stuck imagining every way the conversation could go wrong or how I might come across. And I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep trying to anticipate every possible objection before I send something, even when it’s probably fine. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I know I already touched on this, but it’s very much an everyday thing, not just some rare panic about a specific crisis.

My mind keeps jumping around — contract work, mortgage payments, what people in the Bethesda tech circles might think of me. Like, I’ll be doing something normal and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if they see me as damaged goods now?” professionally, I mean. And then that thought doesn’t stay contained. It turns into, “What if the contract work slows down, what if I can’t cover the mortgage, what if people have already written me off and I just don’t know it yet?”

And the frustrating part is that sometimes nothing has actually happened. Or it’s something tiny, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to reply to an email. Rationally, I know that could mean anything — they’re busy, hiring is slow, somebody’s on vacation. But emotionally it can land like evidence that my whole career is closing down. Like one delayed email becomes this proof that doors are shutting, and I’m already three steps ahead imagining the financial fallout and what people are saying or thinking.

So yes, finances and relationships, or professional relationships especially, I worry about them even when there isn’t an obvious reason right in front of me. It’s like my brain finds the weak spot and just keeps pressing on it.

Soccer is probably the clearest example because that used to be one of the few things that was just social and normal for me — show up, run around, see the guys, have a bit of a laugh afterward. But lately I’ve skipped it more and more because I feel tense before I even go, and I’m embarrassed by it. I worry I’ll come across as distracted or irritable, or someone will ask what’s going on and I won’t know how to answer without sounding like I’m falling apart.\n\nAnd with friends generally, I’ve been less responsive. I tell myself I’m busy or that I’ll get back to them when I’m in a better headspace, but then days go by. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I do. I just feel like I’m not good company right now, and I hate the idea of people seeing me as this uptight, snappy version of myself.\n\nWork has fed into it too, because when I miss networking follow-ups or delay client deliverables because I’m stuck imagining everything that could go wrong, I end up carrying that shame and stress home with me. Then I’m even less likely to reach out to friends or show up for things. So yes, it’s affecting my relationships — maybe not in one dramatic blow-up, but in this slow pulling away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're around others, even if you're not necessarily doing something that involves a lot of social interaction?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. I mean… I don’t think I’ve noticed feeling anxious just from being around people in a general sense, like sitting in a room or being near others automatically sets me off.\n\nIt’s more… I’d have to think about what you mean by “around others.” If I’m just in public or around people but there’s no expectation on me, I don’t know that I’ve paid enough attention to say yes. I tend to notice it more when there’s something attached to it — like I’m supposed to respond, or show up a certain way, or people might notice that I’m tense. But just being around others, by itself… I don’t really have a clear answer. I haven’t thought of it in those terms.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in casual social settings where you don't have to interact or perform in any specific way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t think I’ve really separated it out that clearly in my mind — like, “am I anxious just because I’m casually around people?” versus anxious because there’s some expectation or possible judgment involved.\n\nIf I’m just somewhere casual and I truly don’t have to interact or do anything, I don’t know that I notice it as anxiety exactly. I might still be kind of aware of myself, but I’m not sure if that’s because of the people being there or because I’m already wound up going into it. It’s easier for me to recognize when there’s a chance I’ll have to talk, or someone might ask me something, or I might seem tense or off. But if there’s no real interaction or performance expected… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no answer. I haven’t really paid attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're in a quiet, public place like a library or a park, where you don't have to interact with anyone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. A library or a park… if I’m not expected to talk to anyone or do anything, I don’t know that I would immediately label what I feel as anxiety.\n\nI might still feel a bit on edge, but it’s hard for me to tell whether that’s because I’m in public or because I already arrived feeling wound up from something else. Like, if I’m sitting in a park and no one’s engaging with me, I don’t think the presence of strangers by itself is what I’ve been focusing on. Same with a library — I’m not usually thinking, “Everyone is watching me,” at least not in a clear way that I can point to.\n\nIt’s more noticeable to me when there’s some chance I’ll have to respond or be seen as not handling myself well. But in a quiet public place where there’s really no interaction expected… I don’t have a clean yes or no. I haven’t really paid close enough attention to that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you are alone with your thoughts in a quiet environment?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually more where it happens, I think. When it’s quiet and I’m by myself, there’s nothing to distract me, so my mind just… starts running. And it doesn’t stay on one thing.\n\nLike, I’ll start with something that should be fairly contained — say a client hasn’t responded to an email or a message. At first it’s just, “Okay, why haven’t they gotten back to me?” But then within a few minutes I’m thinking maybe they’re unhappy, maybe we’re going to lose the contract, maybe I missed something important, and then it turns into this whole chain of consequences. If that falls through, then I’m behind financially, then I’m thinking about divorce payments, and then I’m back to thinking I’m never going to get into a real leadership role again or get my life stable the way it used to feel.\n\nIt’s like my brain won’t just stop at the first concern. It keeps building this case against me, one step after another, and I can know on some level that I’m jumping ahead, but that doesn’t make it shut off. That’s honestly a big part of why I came in — because when things get quiet, I can’t seem to turn my thoughts off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced feelings of sadness or hopelessness over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t know that I’d call it sadness exactly, and hopelessness feels like a strong word, so I don’t want to say yes if that’s not what I mean.\n\nOver the past few weeks I’ve had times where I feel pretty worn down or discouraged, but when you say “sad,” I picture something more like crying a lot or feeling grief, and that hasn’t really been what I’ve been focused on. It’s more like I get stuck in my head and then I feel heavy afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nHopelessness… I don’t know. I’ve had moments where things feel like they’re not going the way I want, or like I can’t see a simple path forward, but I’m not sure I’d describe that as feeling hopeless. Maybe frustrated, maybe overwhelmed. I’m having a hard time separating those out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you've failed in some fundamental way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it as “worthless.” That word feels… I don’t know, it feels very absolute, like I’m saying I have no value as a person, and I don’t think that’s exactly how I talk to myself.\n\nFailed in some fundamental way is harder to answer, because I do get into these loops where I look at where I thought I’d be versus where I am, and I can be pretty harsh about it. But I’m not sure if that’s the same as believing I’m fundamentally a failure, or if it’s more that I’m disappointed and I keep measuring myself against some version of my life that I thought I should have had by now.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I often feel worthless. I do feel like I’ve fallen short sometimes, but “failed in a fundamental way” sounds stronger than what I can confidently say. It’s more like questioning myself than a fixed belief, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have your thoughts and avoidance of certain situations or tasks significantly impacted your daily life or relationships over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I wish I could say it was just me worrying internally and still getting everything done, but it’s started spilling over into actual things.\n\nWith work, for example, I’ve missed some networking follow-ups — not because I didn’t care or forgot entirely, but because I’d start thinking through how the conversation might go wrong, or whether I’d sound desperate, or whether they’d judge where I am professionally right now. And then I’d tell myself I’d do it later when I felt clearer, and then later became the next day, and then it just got awkward enough that I avoided it more.\n\nIt’s also affected client work. I’ve delayed deliverables because I get stuck trying to anticipate every possible objection or problem before I send something out. I’ll look at something that’s basically good enough to move forward, but then my mind starts going, “What if they hate this part? What if this creates a bigger issue? What if this makes me look incompetent?” So instead of just finishing it and communicating normally, I sit on it too long.\n\nAnd relationships, yeah. I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Soccer used to be a pretty normal outlet for me, but lately I feel tense going into it, and I’m embarrassed because I know I’ve been more irritable. I don’t really want people noticing that or asking what’s going on, so I just don’t show up as much. Same with friends — I’ve been avoiding plans or keeping things shorter, partly because I don’t want to be in that state around them.\n\nSo yes, over the past few weeks it’s definitely affected my daily life. It’s not just thoughts anymore; it’s causing me to miss things, delay things, and isolate more than I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, that’s actually one of the main things that bothers me, because it doesn’t even always need a real trigger. My mind just starts moving on its own.\n\nIt’ll jump from one thing to another — like I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as, I don’t know, damaged goods professionally. And none of those things may have changed in that exact moment, but my brain treats it like there’s some emergency I’m supposed to solve right then.\n\nEven something small, like a recruiter not emailing back right away, can start to feel like more than just a delayed email. I’ll know logically that people get busy or hiring processes move slowly, but emotionally it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, like doors are shutting and I’m not going to be able to get back to where I should be. And then I’m off into mortgage payments again, and what happens if contract work dries up, and who has noticed, and what they think of me.\n\nSo yes, I do worry when there isn’t an obvious immediate reason. Or maybe there’s a tiny reason, but my mind turns it into a whole chain of consequences before I can stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep, yes. Appetite… I don’t know that I’ve noticed as clearly, but sleep has definitely changed.\n\nMost nights lately, I’ll get into bed and I’m tired, or at least I think I’m tired, but then my mind starts replaying things. A lot of it is interview answers or conversations I’ve had, like I’ll go back over what I said, what I should have said, whether I sounded off or unprepared. And it’s not even productive at that point — it’s just this loop.\n\nThen I end up checking the clock, which I know doesn’t help, but I’ll see it’s 2 or 3 in the morning and then I start doing the math of how much sleep I have left, which just makes it worse. And even on nights where, technically, I get what should be enough hours, I don’t wake up feeling restored. It’s more like I spent the night on call for a system outage — like some part of me was alert the whole time, waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nSo yes, sleep has been a problem over the past few weeks. I wouldn’t say I’m just staying up late by choice. It feels more like I can’t fully shut down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your anxiety symptoms have interfered with your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that are important to you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, the clearest example is soccer, because that used to be one of the few things I could count on enjoying without overthinking it. It was just exercise, seeing people, being part of something that wasn’t work or career stuff. But lately I’ve pulled back from it. I’ll think about going, and then I start imagining myself showing up tense or snapping at someone or just seeming off, and I get embarrassed before I even get there. So instead of going and probably feeling better, I avoid it.\n\nAnd it’s been similar with friends. I’ve been less willing to make plans or I keep things short, because I don’t want them to see how wound up I am. I don’t want to be the irritable guy in the room or have someone ask, “Are you okay?” and then have to explain all of it. So I just sort of disappear a bit, which I know isn’t great.\n\nIt’s also interfering with things that matter professionally. I’ve missed networking follow-ups because I get stuck imagining every way the conversation could go wrong or how I might come across. And I’ve delayed client deliverables because I keep trying to anticipate every possible objection before I send something, even when it’s probably fine. So yes — it’s getting in the way of participating in things I care about, both personally and professionally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like finances or relationships, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know I already touched on this, but it’s very much an everyday thing, not just some rare panic about a specific crisis.\n\nMy mind keeps jumping around — contract work, mortgage payments, what people in the Bethesda tech circles might think of me. Like, I’ll be doing something normal and then suddenly I’m thinking, “What if they see me as damaged goods now?” professionally, I mean. And then that thought doesn’t stay contained. It turns into, “What if the contract work slows down, what if I can’t cover the mortgage, what if people have already written me off and I just don’t know it yet?”\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that sometimes nothing has actually happened. Or it’s something tiny, like a recruiter taking longer than usual to reply to an email. Rationally, I know that could mean anything — they’re busy, hiring is slow, somebody’s on vacation. But emotionally it can land like evidence that my whole career is closing down. Like one delayed email becomes this proof that doors are shutting, and I’m already three steps ahead imagining the financial fallout and what people are saying or thinking.\n\nSo yes, finances and relationships, or professional relationships especially, I worry about them even when there isn’t an obvious reason right in front of me. It’s like my brain finds the weak spot and just keeps pressing on it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Yes. That’s actually a big part of why I came in. I feel like I can’t shut my brain off anymore.

It usually starts with one thing — something that should be manageable, like a client not responding to an email or taking longer than usual to get back to me. And at first I’ll tell myself, okay, they’re probably busy, this doesn’t necessarily mean anything. But then the thought just keeps coming back. I’ll check my phone, reread what I sent, wonder if I phrased something wrong, wonder if they’re unhappy, wonder if they’re about to end the contract.

And then within minutes it’s not just about that email anymore. It becomes this whole chain: if I lose the contract, then I fall behind on payments from the divorce, and if that happens then I’m even further from getting stable again, and then I start thinking I’ll never get back into a real leadership role. Like my brain builds this entire collapse scenario off one unanswered message.

I try to stop it. I’ll tell myself, “You don’t have evidence for this,” or “Think about something else,” but it doesn’t really listen. It’s like the thought has momentum. Even if I push it away for a minute, it circles back around. And the more I try to reason with it, the more it feels like I’m feeding it.
=====================================
